# Hand check of 1,000 credited paragraphs, 2 November 2026

This check measures two things about the paragraphs the site credits to a person: how often the credit names the wrong person, and how often the words differ from the Official Report. It drew 1,000 speech rows with a speaker id at random from the production database on 1 November 2026, after write #10. Two AI readers, one Claude Sonnet and one Claude Opus, judged every row's credit against the stored copy of the Official Report, and a script compared every row's words with it. Royce gave the final verdict on the 60 rows that a reader or the script flagged: 15 row by row, and 45 by ruling on kinds of difference that an agent applied. No person read the 940 rows that both readers and the script passed.

The rules are in [hand-check-rulebook.md](../hand-check-rulebook.md), and the rule for role labels such as "The Chairman" is [ADR 0015](../adr/0015-hand-check-role-credits.md). The site's [ADR 0009](https://github.com/roycehoe/hansard/blob/master/docs/adr/0009-ai-readers-person-judges-flags.md) records the decision that AI readers read every row and a person judges the flags. The draw record is in [hand-check/](hand-check/).

## Result

| Claim | Rows in 1,000 | Ceiling (one-sided exact, 95%) |
|---|---|---|
| Credited wrongly | 4 (2 Tier A, 2 Tier C) | 0.91% |
| Substantive text error | 0 | 0.30% |
| Any error | 7 | 1.31% |

Each ceiling holds at 95% on its own, not jointly. "Any error" adds the 3 cosmetic text errors (Tier C) to the wrong credits; no row has both. Tier A alone is 2 of 1,000, a ceiling of 0.63%. The shares are counts out of 1,000, not weighted.[^weights]

These figures count row 157 (speech 838418) as right. Royce first marked it a Tier A wrong credit, then re-ruled it right on 2 November 2026, after this report was first written, because the packet had shown him the wrong source paragraph (see "Fix status, 2 November 2026"). Before the re-ruling the check had 5 wrong credits (ceiling 1.05%) and 8 errors of any kind (1.44%).

Each reader caught 30 of 30 planted errors, so each catches at least 90.5% of errors of those two kinds (one-sided exact, 95%).

The sample holds no row whose source could not be found and no row whose credit the record cannot settle after Royce's verdicts, so neither adds to the wrong count.

[^weights]: Weighted by each group's share of the frame, the wrong-credit share is 0.40% and the any-error share 0.70%. The five groups' weights differ by at most 0.39%, so weighting moves neither share by more than 0.01 points.

## Older and newer system

The Parliament website moved to a newer system on 10 September 2012. The check reports the two systems apart and gives no figure for a single group of Parliaments.

| Sitting date | Rows | Credited wrongly | Substantive | Any error | Ceiling, credited wrongly | Ceiling, substantive | Ceiling, any error |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Before 10 September 2012 | 532 | 4 | 0 | 5 | 1.71% | 0.56% | 1.97% |
| From 10 September 2012 | 468 | 0 | 0 | 2 | 0.64% | 0.64% | 1.34% |

## The seven errors

| # | Speech | Sitting | What the row shows | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 29 | 242655 | 1965-06-16 | The time stamp "4.10 p.m." is credited to Lim Kim San. | credited wrongly, Tier C (row) |
| 34 | 936385 | 1958-10-08 | The procedural line "Motion and Schedule considered in Committee of Supply." is credited to Chew Swee Kee. | credited wrongly, Tier A (row) |
| 86 | 927784 | 1963-04-09 | The quorum record "Notice taken that thirteen Members were not present ..." is credited to Seow Peck Leng. | credited wrongly, Tier A (row) |
| 446 | 999719 | 2007-09-20 | The italic heading "Return of moneys in management fund and sinking fund" is credited to S Jayakumar. | credited wrongly, Tier C (row) |
| 510 | 876656 | 2009-05-29 | The row puts a space between the italic "bao buey" and the closing quote, which the record prints together. | cosmetic, Tier C (row) |
| 919 | 1522366 | 2025-01-07 | The raised footnote 2 after "seniors" is written "[2]" (register line 71). | cosmetic, Tier C (row) |
| 995 | 1558044 | 2025-11-05 | The row puts a space between two italic runs before "Addressing", which the record prints together. | cosmetic, Tier C (cluster) |

The time stamp row (speech 242655) sits in report 38090, one of the 66 reports write #9 held, which keep rows from an older parser ("The residue behind test 7" in [vision.md](../vision.md)). It is the only error among the 11 sampled rows in held reports.

Row 157 (speech 838418) was on this list as a Tier A wrong credit until Royce re-ruled it right (see "Fix status, 2 November 2026").

### Fix status, 2 November 2026

The verdicts and figures above stay as judged, except row 157, which Royce re-ruled right. Each fix below is in the scraper's code but not yet in the database, so the site still shows every row as it was judged. Counts come from a fresh parse of every report on the replica before and after the fixes, compared row by row. Every changed row is one of the kinds below. A write plan covering all the fixes is built but not applied. The write is Royce's to run.

- **Row 29, speech 242655, the time stamp.** State: fixed in code, not yet in the database. The parser already gives a bare time stamp no row (data-loss register, line 100). The row is still stored because report 38090 has been held since write #9. The write plan releases 38090 alone. Rebuilding it deletes 7 rows: the 5 time stamps, 4 of them credited (ordinals 2, 11, 16, 37 and 71), and 2 heading lines (ordinals 36 and 78). It also moves 6 "Amendment(s) agreed to." lines from a member to no speaker, kind PROCEDURAL (ordinals 46, 53, 61, 70, 75 and 83). No word of speech changes. Commit `4286b51` drops 3 stamp forms the rule missed ("5.45 p. m.", "9.03 p m.", and "7.50 p.m." with its full stop on the next line): 3 rows in 3 other reports. 85 credited stamps stay in 23 other held reports.
- **Row 34, speech 936385, "Motion and Schedule considered in Committee of Supply."** State: fixed in code, not yet in the database. Commit `d9ee171` gives a Committee of Supply record line no speaker: 110 rows in 46 reports. 75 record lines become PROCEDURAL, and 35 rows after them lose the mover's credit (32 "Schedule" captions, 2 "Head" captions that become headings, 1 quoted question). 2 of the 110 sit in report 40380, held since write #9, which keeps its rows.
- **Row 86, speech 927784, the quorum record.** State: fixed in code, not yet in the database. Commit `9d450e7` gives a quorum record no speaker and keeps the turn: 17 rows in 12 reports, and 1 row elsewhere that already had no speaker changes kind only.
- **Row 157, speech 838418, "Sir, I beg to move,".** State: not a site error; packet builder fixed. Royce re-ruled the row right in chat on 2 November 2026, after first marking it a Tier A wrong credit, because the packet showed the wrong source paragraph. Report 34920 prints the line twice. This row is the first, printed under `<b>Mr Chin Harn Tong (Aljunied)</b>(<i>In Mandarin</i>):` and followed by the BAA 1105 amendment, as the stored next row is. The packet builder left the language note in the paragraph's text, so that paragraph did not match exactly, and the packet showed the second, under Mr Hwang Soo Jin. On the record the credit to Chin Harn Tong is right, and the stored rows equal a fresh parse, so the database needs no change. Commit `49617da` makes a language note before the label's colon part of the label. The packets were built before it. Re-matching all 1,030 changes 7: this one, and 6 whose only difference was the language note, which now match exactly.
- **Row 446, speech 999719, the italic heading.** State: fixed in code, not yet in the database. The record closes the italic after a line break, which left the heading's closing `_` alone on the next line. Commit `4e666ff` reads such a line as a heading: 197 rows in 70 reports move from a member to no speaker, kind HEADING, and each gains its closing `_`. One heading before the first label (report 21568) now gets no row, as an italic heading there already does. 5 of the 197 sit in report 23268, held since write #9, which keeps its rows.

Follow-up 5 is built in `5009bf3` (rulebook section 3 rule 6, section 10 and ADR 0015) and `8957ceb` (the packet builder). Rebuilt with it, packet `498bed812370` settles to John Mammen, with "[Mr SPEAKER in the Chair]" shown as a later change of chair, and packet `4b1dd45eed7b` settles to Lim Biow Chuan. A correction to "Two credits Royce found right": the 2019 mark is also printed in the topic text of the CareShield Life and Long-Term Care Bill (report 12330). The packet missed it because it took earlier reports in speech-number order, which put the question-time report and its "Mr Speaker:" after that bill. Follow-up 6 is still open: write #10 has no `docs/figures/` file.

## Who gave each verdict

Both readers gave the same speaker verdict, with the same tier, on all 1,000 rows: 993 right, 5 wrong and 2 "record doesn't say". Their "wrong" on row 157 came from the packet's wrong source paragraph. The script found a difference in 53 rows, none of them among the 7. That makes 60 flags.

Royce's verdicts are in the review page's database, one document per flagged row. Each field records who wrote it: his viewer id, `claude-cluster-ruling`, or `claude-relayed-ruling` for the one verdict he gave in chat. The counts below come from those fields.

| Verdict | Marked by Royce on the row | Set by a cluster ruling | Ruled by Royce in chat |
|---|---|---|---|
| Speaker | 14 | 45 | 1 |
| Text | 12 | 48 | 0 |

Royce marked 15 rows himself: all 7 speaker flags and 8 of the 53 text flags. He later re-ruled one speaker verdict in chat (row 157), so 14 speaker verdicts stand as he marked them on the page. On 3 of those 8 (speeches 99979, 105103 and 550969) his speaker verdict stands and the text verdict comes from a kind ruling below. For the 48 text verdicts he did not mark himself, he read examples of each kind of difference and ruled on the kind, on 2 November 2026:

1. A question number left out of the row is a listed change (data-loss register line 87): 30 rows, including rows he had first marked cosmetic.
2. A language note such as "(In Mandarin):" left out of the row is a listed change (register line 84): 11 rows.
3. A speaker label or its colon left in the source paragraph, or text that sits on the neighbouring row, matches: 6 rows. He had marked 2 rows of this kind himself.
4. An extra space beside italic markers is a cosmetic error, Tier C: 1 row. He had marked the first such row himself.

The coordinating agent sorted the rows into these four kinds and wrote the verdicts as `claude-cluster-ruling`. On the 45 rows where it wrote every field, it also wrote the speaker verdict "right", because both readers said right and the row was not flagged for its speaker. Before this report was written, each cluster-ruled row's script difference was checked against its kind, and all fit. Two rows hold more than the number: in one the source paragraph also holds the previous member's question, which sits on the row before; in the other the label is printed without a colon.

Royce's own marks were saved between 07:16 and 07:51 SGT on 2 November, except his text verdict on row 3 of the review page (speech 851153), which he marked as matching at 08:53 SGT. That row was flagged for its speaker only. The store shows the cluster-ruling writes at 07:53 and 07:55 SGT; their `_at` fields hold a fixed 08:30 SGT stamp rather than the write time. An agent wrote his re-ruling of row 157 at 12:52 SGT, with the writer `claude-relayed-ruling` and the reason in the row's note.

### Two credits Royce found right

Both readers answered "record doesn't say" on two chair labels (review-page rows 3 and 7), and Royce marked both right after reading the whole sitting.

- Speech 851153, "The Chairman", 4 July 1962, credited to John Mammen. The last chair mark before the row is "[Mr DEPUTY SPEAKER (Mr Mammen) in the Chair]", printed at 10.46 p.m. The first mark after it, "[Mr SPEAKER in the Chair]", records a later change, but the rulebook (section 3, rule 6) treats marks before and after that name different people as unsettled.
- Speech 1302702, "Mr Deputy Speaker", 2 September 2019, credited to Lim Biow Chuan. Two Deputy Speakers held office that day. The record prints "[Deputy Speaker (Mr Lim Biow Chuan) in the Chair]" after "Sitting resumed at 4.25 pm", but only in the sitting's full report, not in any topic's text. The packet read the topics' text only, so the nearest mark it found was an earlier "Mr Speaker:".

### Verdicts in a different class from the rulebook's

Royce's verdict is final. Three of his row marks use a different class from the one the rulebook's list of changes would give. Two of them change no figure.

- Speech 1522366: the raised footnote 2 written "[2]". The rulebook classes this as a listed change (register line 71). Royce marked it cosmetic, Tier C, with the note "We should incorporate footnotes, hoverable element". It counts as cosmetic here. As a listed change, cosmetic errors would be 2 and any error 6, a ceiling of 1.18%.
- Speech 1046800, and the 2 cluster rows like it: a chamber event cut out into the next row. The rulebook classes this as a listed change (register line 82); Royce marked it as matching. Neither class is an error.
- Speech 1453228: "life-" and "savings" are split by a column break, and the row joins them with no space. Register line 36 lists that join; Royce marked it as matching. Neither class is an error.

### Answers to Royce's questions

His verdicts stand; these answer the notes that ask something.

- Speech 105103, "Is this really a problem?": no. The parser drops a question number on purpose (register line 87), and his question-number ruling records it as a listed change.
- Speech 550969, whether to label a speech given in another language: see follow-up 2.
- Speech 1046800, "The next line in the OpenHansard is a chamber event for "[interruption]" which is absolutely correct": the site is right. The record prints "[Interruption]." inside Prof. Jayakumar's paragraph, and the parser cuts it out as its own row (register line 82). The script compares each row with the whole source paragraph, so it flagged the two words the row does not hold.
- Speech 1453228, "What's the difference?": a space. The record prints "life- savings", split by a column break, and the row has "life-savings".

Sonnet's suggested classes for the 53 text differences were 47 listed change, 4 substantive and 2 cosmetic. Royce found none of the 4 substantive.

## Side figures

These count Tier C faults that send no row to Royce.

- Rows whose stored text holds a `**` or `__` that the matched source paragraph does not: 0 of 1,000 (user ruling U6).
- Rows holding a single `_`: 13 of 1,000. The text script drops every `_` before it compares, so none shows as a difference. 11 hold matched pairs, which the site shows as italics. 2 hold an unmatched `_` that the site shows as it is: speech 999719, the italic heading among the errors, and speech 875529, which no reader or script flagged. Its source paragraph starts with a stray closing italic tag.
- The gap check found 25 of 1,000 rows not followed by the next source paragraph. It is information only, not a flag.

## Method

**Frame and draw.** The frame is every speech row with a speaker id, 1,002,233 rows, sorted in the database by Parliament, report version, report type, sitting date, SPRS report id, ordinal and speech id, with byte-order collation on the string columns. The draw script (`scripts/draw_hand_check_sample.py`) and its seed, 20261102, were committed before it ran. It read the frame in one read-only, repeatable-read transaction at 20:57 SGT on 1 November 2026. The frame hash is `24af4633ad9f2da7f083bcdb67818c936eb5747b7bca23efc04fef78e008669f`, and an order check over the full frame passed just before the draw. Rows were split into five groups (Parliaments 0 to 11, 12, 13, 14, 15), allocated by largest remainder (515, 83, 133, 207, 62; the brief's 516, 83, 133, 207, 62 sums to 1,001), and drawn by systematic sampling from a seeded start. The 1,000 rows sit in 1,000 different reports. `hand-check/draw.tsv` lists each row's speech id, group and frame position, and `hand-check/draw-summary.txt` the group counts, starts and weight spread.

**Database date.** The draw read the production database after write #10, which committed at 15:43 SGT on 1 November 2026. Write #10 applied the parser fixes `a5ffd43` (appendix captions get no speaker) and `430d99e` (a Resolved line gets no speaker) to 1,285 reports: 1,628 row updates, 1,562 of them a change of speaker, 11 deletes and 269 inserts. Before the draw, a check of 11 rows found the post-write state in the database and on the site's API. Write #10's figures are in `docs/figures/2026-11-02-write10.txt` and its entry is under "The three numbers" in [vision.md](../vision.md). The 66 reports write #9 held stay in the frame; 11 sampled rows sit in them.

**Why not the earlier 50-row page.** An earlier review page of 50 rows drew them through the site's search API. It left eight Parliaments with no rows, used only the first or last 100 reports of each Parliament and skipped written answers. Its 50 marks were saved by an agent within one second, so no person read them. This check drew afresh from the database instead.

**Source.** Every row is compared with the stored copy of the Official Report: `report.content`, the SPRS answer as the scraper fetched it, with a NUL byte as U+FFFD the only change. For 50 sampled reports chosen with a committed seed, the build also fetched today's SPRS text: the stored copy matched live SPRS in 50 of 50.

**Readers.** The 1,000 rows and 30 planted errors became 1,030 packets with random ids, in shuffled batches of at most 50 packets or 80,000 characters, 93 per reader. Each packet shows the row, the source paragraph with the lines up to the nearest speaker label above it, and, for a role label, the office-holder table entries and the sitting's chair marks. Each batch went to one Sonnet and one Opus agent with rulebook section 10 as the prompt, word for word. Neither saw the other's verdicts or any earlier check. One batch was rerun once because a verdict line was missing.

**Planted errors.** 15 packets credit a wrong member and 15 credit a caption, a Resolved line or another procedural line to the member on the row above. The key's SHA-256 (`hand-check/plant-key-round-1.sha256`) was committed before the packets were built, and the key stayed sealed until both readers finished. A reader catches a plant when its speaker verdict is not "right". Both caught all 30, so no rerun round ran.

**Text script.** Both texts are normalised the same way: tags dropped, character references decoded, `**` and every `_` dropped, runs of whitespace folded to one space. A row matches exactly when its text equals one or more consecutive whole source paragraphs. 947 rows matched exactly, 53 did not, and every row's source was found.

**Flags.** A row went to Royce when either reader's speaker verdict was not "right", the script found any difference, or the match was not exact. A row from a live-checked report whose stored copy differed would also have gone; none did.

**Role labels.** 52 sampled rows carry a role label: 36 Speaker, 7 Deputy Speaker, 6 Chairman and 3 Prime Minister. The office-holder table (`data/hand_check_office_holders.json`, each entry cited to an outside source) settled 45, and the sitting's own chair marks settled 5. For the other 2 the readers answered "record doesn't say", and Royce settled both from the full record (above). The check cannot see a change of chair that the record does not print.

**Royce's review.** A private review page listed the 60 flagged rows, each as its packet with the Official Report link, and saved each mark with his viewer id and the time.

## Live site check

On 2 November 2026 each sampled report was fetched once from the site's API, with a new cache key each time and at least 2 seconds between requests. All 1,000 answered 200. In all 1,000 the row at the snapshot's ordinal has the snapshot's label, credited person, kind and text, so the site shows each row as it was judged.

Each site link opens the row on its report page, built from the API's `report_slug` and the row's `anchor` as the site's own "Copy link" button builds it.

## Follow-ups

None of these was built when the check was written. Follow-ups 1 and 5 were built on 2 November 2026; see "Fix status, 2 November 2026".

1. **Procedural lines still credited.** Royce's note on speeches 936385 and 927784: "This should be a procedural, but I have no idea how to mark this programatically". The rows are "Motion and Schedule considered in Committee of Supply." (8 October 1958) and "Notice taken that thirteen Members were not present, Members summoned as if for a division pursuant to the Standing Order, and meeting resumed upon a quorum being then present." (9 April 1963). Both are the clerk's record of what the Assembly did, not anyone's words: a whole paragraph of its own after a member's turn, in the past tense and the passive, with no "I", "we", "Sir" or form of address, naming the House's business (a motion, a schedule, a notice, Members) as the subject. Both are close kin of lines the parser already gives no speaker: "Bill considered in Committee" in `_PROCEDURAL_LINE_ANCHORS` and "Members summoned into (the) Chamber" in `_PROCEDURAL_RECORD_LINE_RE` (`services/speech.py`). Proposed rule: add anchors for "(Motion|Bill)( and Schedule)? (considered in|committed to) (the )?Committee of Supply", "Notice taken that ... Members were not present" and "Members summoned as if for a division", count the stored rows each would change before writing them, and add a register row. The line before the 1958 row, "Motion committed to Committee of Supply.", is the same kind.
2. **Language notes kept as data.** Royce's note on speech 550969: the language a speech was given in should be kept on its row and shown as a raised mark on the site that explains itself on hover. The parser now strips the note (register line 84), so this needs a column for the language and a write to fill it.
3. **Footnotes as a hover element.** Royce's note on speech 1522366: "We should incorporate footnotes, hoverable element". The site shows a footnote number as "[2]" (register line 71) and the footnote's text as its own row.
4. **Single `_` markers.** Royce's note on speech 876656: "I think we are not processing italics properly". The row has "_bao buey_ " with a space before the closing quote that the record prints straight after the italics; speech 1558044 has the same fault. The site shows a matched `_..._` pair as italics, so "[_Applause_.]" reads correctly on a report page, but the markers stay in the API's text, and an unmatched `_` shows on the page as it is (speech 875529). The U6 count looks only for `**` and `__`. A fuller count should cover a single `_` left unmatched, and a space the row adds next to an italic marker.
5. **Chair marks.** Two rule changes would have let the readers settle both chair rows above. First, in the newer system, read the chair marks from the sitting's full text (`sitting.sections`), which stage 8's sitting-level Deputy Speaker pass already reads, as well as from the topics' text. Second, in rulebook section 3 rule 6 and ADR 0015, treat a "[X in the Chair]" note or a "took the Chair" line after the row as a later change, not a conflict, so the last mark before the row decides. A presiding label after the row that names someone else, with no change note between, would still leave the credit unsettled.
6. **Write #10's record.** The scraper's `CLAUDE.md` asks every write to end with a `docs/figures/` file and an entry in `docs/vision.md`. Write #10 had neither when the check was written. Both were added on 2 November 2026: `docs/figures/2026-11-02-write10.txt` and an entry under "The three numbers" in `docs/vision.md`.

## All 1,000 rows

Rows are in draw order: by group, then frame position. "Speech" is the speech id, as in `hand-check/draw.tsv`. Sonnet and Opus give each reader's speaker verdict, with the tier of a wrong credit. A row with Royce's verdicts was flagged. "row" means he marked that field on the row, "cluster" that one of his rulings above set it, and "chat" that he ruled it in chat and an agent wrote it to the review page. A row with no Royce verdict was not flagged: both readers said right and the script found its text equal to the record's.
